People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. I’ll try to tell myself the numbers matched, I saw it, but it comes right back, like I can’t trust my own eyes.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs this need to check and recheck something that happens only with your work, or do you find yourself doing similar things in other parts of your life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 23 / bookkeeping_accounting_or_auditing_clerk / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Compulsions\nmanifestation: \"I'm here because I can't stop going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations again and again, sometimes staying at work for hours after everyone leaves, even when I already checked them, because if I don't do it one more time I feel like something bad will happen and it'll be my fault.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t seem to stop checking my work. I do bookkeeping, and I keep going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations again and again, even when I know I already checked them. Sometimes I stay at work for hours after everyone else leaves because I feel like if I don’t check just one more time, something bad will happen and it’ll be my fault.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you're not actively checking your work?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even when I’m not checking right then, the thought keeps popping up that I missed some hidden mistake—like in payroll or taxes—and then someone’s check won’t go through or the firm will get in trouble. I’ll try to tell myself the numbers matched, I saw it, but it comes right back, like I can’t trust my own eyes.\n\nDoctor: Is this need to check and recheck something that happens only with your work, or do you find yourself doing similar things in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly with work. The spreadsheets and calculations are where I get stuck. I’ll check them, and then I’ll go back and check again, and sometimes I’m still there hours after everyone else leaves. I know I already looked, but it feels like if I don’t do it one more time, something bad could happen and it would be my fault.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these repeated checks cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s really upsetting. I know I’ve already checked the spreadsheet or the calculations, but I still feel like I have to go back one more time. If I don’t, I get this strong feeling that something bad will happen and it’ll be my fault. It gets bad enough that I’ll stay at work for hours after everyone else leaves, just checking the same things over and over.\n\nDoctor: Are these checking behaviors affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and some youth-center commitments because I can’t get out of the office on time. And at work it’s gotten bad too—I missed a filing deadline and got called into a formal meeting about it. It’s embarrassing, and I feel like I’m letting people down.\n\nDoctor: What happens when you're unable to check your work one more time, do you feel a significant amount of anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes. If I can’t check it again, I get really anxious. Even if I know I already went over the spreadsheet or the calculations, it feels like I’m leaving something dangerous unfinished. I keep thinking that if something goes wrong, it’ll be my fault, so I end up going back over it again and again.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about missing a mistake in your work feel like they are coming from outside of yourself, or are they more like your own worries?\n\nPatient: They feel like my own worries, not like someone is putting them in my head or anything. It’s more like… even after I know the numbers match, this thought jumps in that I missed some hidden mistake in payroll or taxes, and then I start thinking someone’s check won’t go through or the firm will get in trouble. I try to push it away, but it comes right back, like I can’t trust what I just checked.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I try to push it away, but it comes right back, like I can’t trust what I just checked.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeating thoughts always about preventing some kind of mistake or harm from happening?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t seem to stop checking my work. I do bookkeeping, and I keep going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations again and again, even when I know I already checked them. Sometimes I stay at work for hours after everyone else leaves because I feel like if I don’t check just one more time, something bad will happen and it’ll be my fault.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you're not actively checking your work?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even when I’m not checking right then, the thought keeps popping up that I missed some hidden mistake—like in payroll or taxes—and then someone’s check won’t go through or the firm will get in trouble. I’ll try to tell myself the numbers matched, I saw it, but it comes right back, like I can’t trust my own eyes.\n\nDoctor: Is this need to check and recheck something that happens only with your work, or do you find yourself doing similar things in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly with work. The spreadsheets and calculations are where I get stuck. I’ll check them, and then I’ll go back and check again, and sometimes I’m still there hours after everyone else leaves. I know I already looked, but it feels like if I don’t do it one more time, something bad could happen and it would be my fault.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these repeated checks cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s really upsetting. I know I’ve already checked the spreadsheet or the calculations, but I still feel like I have to go back one more time. If I don’t, I get this strong feeling that something bad will happen and it’ll be my fault. It gets bad enough that I’ll stay at work for hours after everyone else leaves, just checking the same things over and over.\n\nDoctor: Are these checking behaviors affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and some youth-center commitments because I can’t get out of the office on time. And at work it’s gotten bad too—I missed a filing deadline and got called into a formal meeting about it. It’s embarrassing, and I feel like I’m letting people down.\n\nDoctor: What happens when you're unable to check your work one more time, do you feel a significant amount of anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes. If I can’t check it again, I get really anxious. Even if I know I already went over the spreadsheet or the calculations, it feels like I’m leaving something dangerous unfinished. I keep thinking that if something goes wrong, it’ll be my fault, so I end up going back over it again and again.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about missing a mistake in your work feel like they are coming from outside of yourself, or are they more like your own worries?\n\nPatient: They feel like my own worries, not like someone is putting them in my head or anything. It’s more like… even after I know the numbers match, this thought jumps in that I missed some hidden mistake in payroll or taxes, and then I start thinking someone’s check won’t go through or the firm will get in trouble. I try to push it away, but it comes right back, like I can’t trust what I just checked.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeating thoughts always about preventing some kind of mistake or harm from happening?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes. It’s usually about me missing one hidden mistake in the payroll or taxes, even when I already know the numbers match. I’ll try to push the thought away, but it comes right back, like I can’t trust my own eyes, and then I start thinking somebody’s check won’t go through or the firm will get in trouble.\n\nDoctor: Are your checks always aimed at preventing something specific from happening, like a financial error or legal trouble?\n\nPatient: Yes, usually. It’s like I’m checking to stop something bad from happening. I keep going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations, even when I already checked them, because if I don’t do it one more time, I feel like I might miss something and it’ll be my fault. Sometimes I stay at work for hours after everyone else leaves just checking again.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to perform these checks in a specific way or order to feel right, even if it doesn't necessarily relate to preventing a mistake?\n\nPatient: Not really in a special order, no. It gets bad enough that I’ll stay at work for hours after everyone else leaves, just checking the same things over and over.\n\nDoctor: Are these checking behaviors affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and some youth-center commitments because I can’t get out of the office on time. And at work it’s gotten bad too—I missed a filing deadline and got called into a formal meeting about it. It’s embarrassing, and I feel like I’m letting people down.\n\nDoctor: What happens when you're unable to check your work one more time, do you feel a significant amount of anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes. If I can’t check it again, I get really anxious. Even if I know I already went over the spreadsheet or the calculations, it feels like I’m leaving something dangerous unfinished. I keep thinking that if something goes wrong, it’ll be my fault, so I end up going back over it again and again.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about missing a mistake in your work feel like they are coming from outside of yourself, or are they more like your own worries?\n\nPatient: They feel like my own worries, not like someone is putting them in my head or anything. It’s more like… even after I know the numbers match, this thought jumps in that I missed some hidden mistake in payroll or taxes, and then I start thinking someone’s check won’t go through or the firm will get in trouble. I try to push it away, but it comes right back, like I can’t trust what I just checked.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeating thoughts always about preventing some kind of mistake or harm from happening?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes. It’s usually about me missing one hidden mistake in the payroll or taxes, even when I already know the numbers match. I’ll try to push the thought away, but it comes right back, like I can’t trust my own eyes, and then I start thinking somebody’s check won’t go through or the firm will get in trouble.\n\nDoctor: Are your checks always aimed at preventing something specific from happening, like a financial error or legal trouble?\n\nPatient: Yes, usually. It’s like I’m checking to stop something bad from happening. I keep going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations, even when I already checked them, because if I don’t do it one more time, I feel like I might miss something and it’ll be my fault. Sometimes I stay at work for hours after everyone else leaves just checking again.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to perform these checks in a specific way or order to feel right, even if it doesn't necessarily relate to preventing a mistake?\n\nPatient: Not really in a special order, no. It’s more that I feel like I have to go back over the spreadsheets and calculations again and again. Even if I already checked them, I’ll think, “If I don’t do it one more time, something bad could happen and it’ll be my fault.” Sometimes I end up staying at work for hours after everyone leaves just checking the same things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your work again because you feel compelled to, even if you know it's already correct?\n\nPatient: Yes. I know it’s already correct most of the time, but I still feel like I have to check it again. I’ll go back over the same spreadsheets and calculations over and over, and if I try to stop, I get this feeling that something bad might happen and it’ll be my fault. Sometimes I stay at work for hours after everyone leaves because I can’t let it go.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

It’s more like… even after I know the numbers match, this thought jumps in that I missed some hidden mistake in payroll or taxes, and then I start thinking someone’s check won’t go through or the firm will get in trouble. I try to push it away, but it comes right back, like I can’t trust what I just checked.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeating thoughts always about preventing some kind of mistake or harm from happening?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes. It’s usually about me missing one hidden mistake in the payroll or taxes, even when I already know the numbers match. I’ll try to push the thought away, but it comes right back, like I can’t trust my own eyes, and then I start thinking somebody’s check won’t go through or the firm will get in trouble.\n\nDoctor: Are your checks always aimed at preventing something specific from happening, like a financial error or legal trouble?\n\nPatient: Yes, usually. It’s like I’m checking to stop something bad from happening. I keep going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations, even when I already checked them, because if I don’t do it one more time, I feel like I might miss something and it’ll be my fault. Sometimes I stay at work for hours after everyone else leaves just checking again.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to perform these checks in a specific way or order to feel right, even if it doesn't necessarily relate to preventing a mistake?\n\nPatient: Not really in a special order, no. It’s more that I feel like I have to go back over the spreadsheets and calculations again and again. Even if I already checked them, I’ll think, “If I don’t do it one more time, something bad could happen and it’ll be my fault.” Sometimes I end up staying at work for hours after everyone leaves just checking the same things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your work again because you feel compelled to, even if you know it's already correct?\n\nPatient: Yes. I know it’s already correct most of the time, but I still feel like I have to check it again. I’ll go back over the same spreadsheets and calculations over and over, and if I try to stop, I get this feeling that something bad might happen and it’ll be my fault. Sometimes I stay at work for hours after everyone leaves because I can’t let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checks need to be done in a way that feels 'just right' to you?\n\nPatient: Not really “just right,” I don’t think. It’s more like I can’t stop myself from checking the spreadsheets and calculations again. Even if I already checked them, I feel like if I don’t do it one more time, something bad could happen and it would be my fault. Sometimes I end up staying for hours after everyone else leaves because I keep going back over it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

{"intent": "Determine if the patient's checking behavior is driven by a need for symmetry or exactness", "question": "Do you feel like your checks are complete only when you've done them a certain number of times?"}

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t seem to stop checking my work. I do bookkeeping, and I keep going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations again and again, even when I know I already checked them. Sometimes I stay at work for hours after everyone else leaves because I feel like if I don’t check just one more time, something bad will happen and it’ll be my fault.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you're not actively checking your work?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even when I’m not checking right then, the thought keeps popping up that I missed some hidden mistake—like in payroll or taxes—and then someone’s check won’t go through or the firm will get in trouble. I’ll try to tell myself the numbers matched, I saw it, but it comes right back, like I can’t trust my own eyes.\n\nDoctor: Is this need to check and recheck something that happens only with your work, or do you find yourself doing similar things in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly with work. The spreadsheets and calculations are where I get stuck. I’ll check them, and then I’ll go back and check again, and sometimes I’m still there hours after everyone else leaves. I know I already looked, but it feels like if I don’t do it one more time, something bad could happen and it would be my fault.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these repeated checks cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s really upsetting. I know I’ve already checked the spreadsheet or the calculations, but I still feel like I have to go back one more time. If I don’t, I get this strong feeling that something bad will happen and it’ll be my fault. It gets bad enough that I’ll stay at work for hours after everyone else leaves, just checking the same things over and over.\n\nDoctor: Are these checking behaviors affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and some youth-center commitments because I can’t get out of the office on time. And at work it’s gotten bad too—I missed a filing deadline and got called into a formal meeting about it. It’s embarrassing, and I feel like I’m letting people down.\n\nDoctor: What happens when you're unable to check your work one more time, do you feel a significant amount of anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes. If I can’t check it again, I get really anxious. Even if I know I already went over the spreadsheet or the calculations, it feels like I’m leaving something dangerous unfinished. I keep thinking that if something goes wrong, it’ll be my fault, so I end up going back over it again and again.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about missing a mistake in your work feel like they are coming from outside of yourself, or are they more like your own worries?\n\nPatient: They feel like my own worries, not like someone is putting them in my head or anything. Even if I already checked them, I feel like if I don’t do it one more time, something bad could happen and it would be my fault. Sometimes I end up staying for hours after everyone else leaves because I keep going back over it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checks are complete only when you've done them a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Not really a certain number, no. Sometimes that keeps me there for hours.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Sometimes I stay at work for hours after everyone leaves because I can’t let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checks need to be done in a way that feels 'just right' to you?\n\nPatient: Not really “just right,” I don’t think. It’s more like I can’t stop myself from checking the spreadsheets and calculations again. Even if I already checked them, I feel like if I don’t do it one more time, something bad could happen and it would be my fault. Sometimes I end up staying for hours after everyone else leaves because I keep going back over it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checks are complete only when you've done them a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Not really a certain number, no. It’s more like I’ll tell myself, “just one more time,” but then after I check it, I still don’t feel okay. So I go back over the same spreadsheet or calculation again because I feel like if I stop too soon and something is wrong, something bad could happen and it would be my fault. Sometimes that keeps me there for hours.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your work or the way you check things has to be symmetrical or perfect in some way?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t seem to stop checking my work. I do bookkeeping, and I keep going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations again and again, even when I know I already checked them. Sometimes I stay at work for hours after everyone else leaves because I feel like if I don’t check just one more time, something bad will happen and it’ll be my fault.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you're not actively checking your work?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even when I’m not checking right then, the thought keeps popping up that I missed some hidden mistake—like in payroll or taxes—and then someone’s check won’t go through or the firm will get in trouble. I’ll try to tell myself the numbers matched, I saw it, but it comes right back, like I can’t trust my own eyes.\n\nDoctor: Is this need to check and recheck something that happens only with your work, or do you find yourself doing similar things in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly with work. The spreadsheets and calculations are where I get stuck. I’ll check them, and then I’ll go back and check again, and sometimes I’m still there hours after everyone else leaves. I know I already looked, but it feels like if I don’t do it one more time, something bad could happen and it would be my fault.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these repeated checks cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s really upsetting. I know I’ve already checked the spreadsheet or the calculations, but I still feel like I have to go back one more time. If I don’t, I get this strong feeling that something bad will happen and it’ll be my fault. It gets bad enough that I’ll stay at work for hours after everyone else leaves, just checking the same things over and over.\n\nDoctor: Are these checking behaviors affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and some youth-center commitments because I can’t get out of the office on time. And at work it’s gotten bad too—I missed a filing deadline and got called into a formal meeting about it. It’s embarrassing, and I feel like I’m letting people down.\n\nDoctor: What happens when you're unable to check your work one more time, do you feel a significant amount of anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes. If I can’t check it again, I get really anxious. Even if I know I already went over the spreadsheet or the calculations, it feels like I’m leaving something dangerous unfinished. I keep thinking that if something goes wrong, it’ll be my fault, so I end up going back over it again and again.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about missing a mistake in your work feel like they are coming from outside of yourself, or are they more like your own worries?\n\nPatient: They feel like my own worries, not like someone is putting them in my head or anything. It’s more like… even after I know the numbers match, this thought jumps in that I missed some hidden mistake in payroll or taxes, and then I start thinking someone’s check won’t go through or the firm will get in trouble. I try to push it away, but it comes right back, like I can’t trust what I just checked.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeating thoughts always about preventing some kind of mistake or harm from happening?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes. It’s usually about me missing one hidden mistake in the payroll or taxes, even when I already know the numbers match. I’ll try to push the thought away, but it comes right back, like I can’t trust my own eyes, and then I start thinking somebody’s check won’t go through or the firm will get in trouble.\n\nDoctor: Are your checks always aimed at preventing something specific from happening, like a financial error or legal trouble?\n\nPatient: Yes, usually. It’s like I’m checking to stop something bad from happening. I keep going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations, even when I already checked them, because if I don’t do it one more time, I feel like I might miss something and it’ll be my fault. Sometimes I stay at work for hours after everyone else leaves just checking again.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to perform these checks in a specific way or order to feel right, even if it doesn't necessarily relate to preventing a mistake?\n\nPatient: Not really in a special order, no. It’s more that I feel like I have to go back over the spreadsheets and calculations again and again. Even if I already checked them, I’ll think, “If I don’t do it one more time, something bad could happen and it’ll be my fault.” Sometimes I end up staying at work for hours after everyone leaves just checking the same things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your work again because you feel compelled to, even if you know it's already correct?\n\nPatient: Yes. I know it’s already correct most of the time, but I still feel like I have to check it again. I’ll go back over the same spreadsheets and calculations over and over, and if I try to stop, I get this feeling that something bad might happen and it’ll be my fault. Sometimes I stay at work for hours after everyone leaves because I can’t let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checks need to be done in a way that feels 'just right' to you?\n\nPatient: Not really “just right,” I don’t think. It’s more like I can’t stop myself from checking the spreadsheets and calculations again. Even if I already checked them, I feel like if I don’t do it one more time, something bad could happen and it would be my fault. Sometimes I end up staying for hours after everyone else leaves because I keep going back over it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checks are complete only when you've done them a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Not really a certain number, no. It’s more like I’ll tell myself, “just one more time,” but then after I check it, I still don’t feel okay. So I go back over the same spreadsheet or calculation again because I feel like if I stop too soon and something is wrong, something bad could happen and it would be my fault. Sometimes that keeps me there for hours.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your work or the way you check things has to be symmetrical or perfect in some way?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s about being symmetrical or perfect like that. I haven’t really noticed needing things to look a certain way. It’s more just... I feel like I have to check, but not because it has to be arranged perfectly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess maybe, but it doesn’t feel like control exactly. It’s more that when I’m uncertain, I can’t stop going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I can’t seem to stop checking my work. I do bookkeeping, and I keep going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations again and again, even when I know I already checked them. Sometimes I stay at work for hours after everyone else leaves because I feel like if I don’t check just one more time, something bad will happen and it’ll be my fault.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you're not actively checking your work?\n\nPatient: Yes. Even when I’m not checking right then, the thought keeps popping up that I missed some hidden mistake—like in payroll or taxes—and then someone’s check won’t go through or the firm will get in trouble. I’ll try to tell myself the numbers matched, I saw it, but it comes right back, like I can’t trust my own eyes.\n\nDoctor: Is this need to check and recheck something that happens only with your work, or do you find yourself doing similar things in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly with work. The spreadsheets and calculations are where I get stuck. I’ll check them, and then I’ll go back and check again, and sometimes I’m still there hours after everyone else leaves. I know I already looked, but it feels like if I don’t do it one more time, something bad could happen and it would be my fault.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these repeated checks cause you?\n\nPatient: It’s really upsetting. I know I’ve already checked the spreadsheet or the calculations, but I still feel like I have to go back one more time. If I don’t, I get this strong feeling that something bad will happen and it’ll be my fault. It gets bad enough that I’ll stay at work for hours after everyone else leaves, just checking the same things over and over.\n\nDoctor: Are these checking behaviors affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and some youth-center commitments because I can’t get out of the office on time. And at work it’s gotten bad too—I missed a filing deadline and got called into a formal meeting about it. It’s embarrassing, and I feel like I’m letting people down.\n\nDoctor: What happens when you're unable to check your work one more time, do you feel a significant amount of anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes. If I can’t check it again, I get really anxious. Even if I know I already went over the spreadsheet or the calculations, it feels like I’m leaving something dangerous unfinished. I keep thinking that if something goes wrong, it’ll be my fault, so I end up going back over it again and again.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about missing a mistake in your work feel like they are coming from outside of yourself, or are they more like your own worries?\n\nPatient: They feel like my own worries, not like someone is putting them in my head or anything. It’s more like… even after I know the numbers match, this thought jumps in that I missed some hidden mistake in payroll or taxes, and then I start thinking someone’s check won’t go through or the firm will get in trouble. I try to push it away, but it comes right back, like I can’t trust what I just checked.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeating thoughts always about preventing some kind of mistake or harm from happening?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes. It’s usually about me missing one hidden mistake in the payroll or taxes, even when I already know the numbers match. I’ll try to push the thought away, but it comes right back, like I can’t trust my own eyes, and then I start thinking somebody’s check won’t go through or the firm will get in trouble.\n\nDoctor: Are your checks always aimed at preventing something specific from happening, like a financial error or legal trouble?\n\nPatient: Yes, usually. It’s like I’m checking to stop something bad from happening. I keep going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations, even when I already checked them, because if I don’t do it one more time, I feel like I might miss something and it’ll be my fault. Sometimes I stay at work for hours after everyone else leaves just checking again.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to perform these checks in a specific way or order to feel right, even if it doesn't necessarily relate to preventing a mistake?\n\nPatient: Not really in a special order, no. It’s more that I feel like I have to go back over the spreadsheets and calculations again and again. Even if I already checked them, I’ll think, “If I don’t do it one more time, something bad could happen and it’ll be my fault.” Sometimes I end up staying at work for hours after everyone leaves just checking the same things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your work again because you feel compelled to, even if you know it's already correct?\n\nPatient: Yes. I know it’s already correct most of the time, but I still feel like I have to check it again. I’ll go back over the same spreadsheets and calculations over and over, and if I try to stop, I get this feeling that something bad might happen and it’ll be my fault. Sometimes I stay at work for hours after everyone leaves because I can’t let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checks need to be done in a way that feels 'just right' to you?\n\nPatient: Not really “just right,” I don’t think. It’s more like I can’t stop myself from checking the spreadsheets and calculations again. Even if I already checked them, I feel like if I don’t do it one more time, something bad could happen and it would be my fault. Sometimes I end up staying for hours after everyone else leaves because I keep going back over it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checks are complete only when you've done them a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Not really a certain number, no. It’s more like I’ll tell myself, “just one more time,” but then after I check it, I still don’t feel okay. So I go back over the same spreadsheet or calculation again because I feel like if I stop too soon and something is wrong, something bad could happen and it would be my fault. Sometimes that keeps me there for hours.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your work or the way you check things has to be symmetrical or perfect in some way?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s about being symmetrical or perfect like that. I haven’t really noticed needing things to look a certain way. It’s more just... I feel like I have to check, but not because it has to be arranged perfectly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your work is a way to regain control when you feel overwhelmed or uncertain?\n\nPatient: I guess maybe, but it doesn’t feel like control exactly. It’s more that when I’m uncertain, I can’t stop going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations. Even if I already checked them, I feel like I have to do it one more time, because if I don’t and something bad happens, it’ll be my fault. Sometimes I end up staying at work for hours after everyone else leaves.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I know I’ve already checked the spreadsheet or the calculations, but I still feel like I have to go back one more time. If I don’t, I get this strong feeling that something bad will happen and it’ll be my fault. It gets bad enough that I’ll stay at work for hours after everyone else leaves, just checking the same things over and over.\n\nDoctor: Are these checking behaviors affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, it is. I’ve stopped making it to choir rehearsals and some youth-center commitments because I can’t get out of the office on time. And at work it’s gotten bad too—I missed a filing deadline and got called into a formal meeting about it. It’s embarrassing, and I feel like I’m letting people down.\n\nDoctor: What happens when you're unable to check your work one more time, do you feel a significant amount of anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes. If I can’t check it again, I get really anxious. Even if I know I already went over the spreadsheet or the calculations, it feels like I’m leaving something dangerous unfinished. I keep thinking that if something goes wrong, it’ll be my fault, so I end up going back over it again and again.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts about missing a mistake in your work feel like they are coming from outside of yourself, or are they more like your own worries?\n\nPatient: They feel like my own worries, not like someone is putting them in my head or anything. It’s more like… even after I know the numbers match, this thought jumps in that I missed some hidden mistake in payroll or taxes, and then I start thinking someone’s check won’t go through or the firm will get in trouble. I try to push it away, but it comes right back, like I can’t trust what I just checked.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeating thoughts always about preventing some kind of mistake or harm from happening?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes. It’s usually about me missing one hidden mistake in the payroll or taxes, even when I already know the numbers match. I’ll try to push the thought away, but it comes right back, like I can’t trust my own eyes, and then I start thinking somebody’s check won’t go through or the firm will get in trouble.\n\nDoctor: Are your checks always aimed at preventing something specific from happening, like a financial error or legal trouble?\n\nPatient: Yes, usually. It’s like I’m checking to stop something bad from happening. I keep going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations, even when I already checked them, because if I don’t do it one more time, I feel like I might miss something and it’ll be my fault. Sometimes I stay at work for hours after everyone else leaves just checking again.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to perform these checks in a specific way or order to feel right, even if it doesn't necessarily relate to preventing a mistake?\n\nPatient: Not really in a special order, no. It’s more that I feel like I have to go back over the spreadsheets and calculations again and again. Even if I already checked them, I’ll think, “If I don’t do it one more time, something bad could happen and it’ll be my fault.” Sometimes I end up staying at work for hours after everyone leaves just checking the same things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your work again because you feel compelled to, even if you know it's already correct?\n\nPatient: Yes. I know it’s already correct most of the time, but I still feel like I have to check it again. I’ll go back over the same spreadsheets and calculations over and over, and if I try to stop, I get this feeling that something bad might happen and it’ll be my fault. Sometimes I stay at work for hours after everyone leaves because I can’t let it go.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checks need to be done in a way that feels 'just right' to you?\n\nPatient: Not really “just right,” I don’t think. It’s more like I can’t stop myself from checking the spreadsheets and calculations again. Even if I already checked them, I feel like if I don’t do it one more time, something bad could happen and it would be my fault. Sometimes I end up staying for hours after everyone else leaves because I keep going back over it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checks are complete only when you've done them a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Not really a certain number, no. It’s more like I’ll tell myself, “just one more time,” but then after I check it, I still don’t feel okay. So I go back over the same spreadsheet or calculation again because I feel like if I stop too soon and something is wrong, something bad could happen and it would be my fault. Sometimes that keeps me there for hours.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your work or the way you check things has to be symmetrical or perfect in some way?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s about being symmetrical or perfect like that. I haven’t really noticed needing things to look a certain way. It’s more just... I feel like I have to check, but not because it has to be arranged perfectly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking your work is a way to regain control when you feel overwhelmed or uncertain?\n\nPatient: I guess maybe, but it doesn’t feel like control exactly. It’s more that when I’m uncertain, I can’t stop going back over the same spreadsheets and calculations. Even if I already checked them, I feel like I have to do it one more time, because if I don’t and something bad happens, it’ll be my fault. Sometimes I end up staying at work for hours after everyone else leaves.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking is driven more by the fear of something going wrong or by the desire to make sure everything is perfect?\n\nPatient: It’s more fear something will go wrong. Even when the numbers match, I keep getting this thought that maybe I missed some hidden mistake in payroll or taxes, and then someone’s check won’t go through or the firm will get in trouble. I try to push it away, but it comes right back, like I can’t trust what I just saw.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It’s like I’m checking to stop something bad from happening. Sometimes I stay at work for hours after everyone else leaves just checking again.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you need to perform these checks in a specific way or order to feel right, even if it doesn't necessarily relate to preventing a mistake?\n\nPatient: Not really in a special order, no. Even if I already checked them, I feel like if I don’t do it one more time, something bad could happen and it would be my fault. Sometimes I end up staying for hours after everyone else leaves because I keep going back over it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checks are complete only when you've done them a certain number of times?\n\nPatient: Not really a certain number, no. It’s more like I’ll tell myself, “just one more time,” but then after I check it, I still don’t feel okay.
